<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We construct, in deterministic polynomial bit time, a polynomial-size list of rational matrix tuples for noncommutative rational formulas over ℚ of bounded tree size. Every nonzero admissible formula has a defined, invertible value at one tuple, with no separate bounds on inverse nesting or rational constant heights. Matrix dimensions, entry bit lengths, and total output length are polynomially bounded.

- **claim-main-hitting-list** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There are a deterministic Turing machine $G$ and fixed positive integers $C,k$ with the following property. Given $1^n,1^s$, where $n,s\geq1$, the machine outputs a finite list $\mathcal H_{n,s}$ of $n$-tuples of rational square matrices, in at most $C(n+s+1)^k$ bit operations. The entire output, with signed binary numerators and positive binary denominators, has at most $C(n+s+1)^k$ bits. For every nonzero admissible rational formula $\Phi$ in $n$ variables of size at most $s$, some tuple in $\mathcal H_{n,s}$ is a defined evaluation of $\Phi$ with invertible value.* *The same list works for all such formulas, regardless of the bit lengths of their rational constants or their inverse depth. The generator receives no formula, oracle, randomness, or advice.
  - Explanation: A single deterministic machine, given only the unary bounds, writes a polynomial-size list of rational matrix tuples. Every nonzero admissible formula of the stated size has a defined evaluation with invertible value at some tuple. The list is independent of rational constant heights and inverse depth.
- **claim-rectangular-rank** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-rectangular)): Let $1\leq q\leq B$ and $A_0,\ldots,A_n\in\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_q(\mathbb Q)$. Suppose that, for some extension field $E/\mathbb Q$, some integer $e\geq1$, and some $X_1,\ldots,X_n\in\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_e(E)$, the matrix $$A_0\otimes I_e+\sum_{i=1}^n A_i\otimes X_i$$ is invertible. Then $\sum_{i=0}^n A_i\otimes T_i$ is injective over $F$, and $$\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}_{\mathbb Q(t)}\left(\sum_{i=0}^n A_i\otimes R_i(t)\right) \geq qN>(q-1)M. \label{eq:padded-rank}$$
  - Explanation: If a bounded-order pencil has an invertible evaluation over some extension field, its evaluation on the rectangular test operators is injective. The padded square version then has rank strictly above that of a deficient block.
- **claim-wronskian-orders** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-wronskian-orders)): With the notation above, at the $w=n+1$ points $0,\ldots,n$ one has $$\sum_{i=0}^n\sum_{j=1}^h d_{ij} \leq h(d-1)+w\binom{h}{2}. \label{eq:wronskian-bound}$$
  - Explanation: The total of the vanishing orders of a polynomial subspace over all expansion points is bounded by the degree of its Wronskian.
- **claim-integral-reduction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-integral-reduction)): Every $k$-dimensional subspace $U\subseteq F^D$ has a basis $u_1,\ldots,u_k\in\mathcal O^D$ whose reductions are linearly independent. For such a basis, $$U\cap\mathcal O^D=\bigoplus_{j=1}^k\mathcal O u_j, \qquad \dim_{\mathbb Q}\overline U=k. \label{eq:integral-lattice}$$
  - Explanation: Every finite-dimensional subspace over formal Laurent series has an integral basis whose reductions stay independent, so the reduced space keeps the full dimension.
- **claim-small-subspace-expansion** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-small-expansion)): For every $k$-dimensional subspace $U\subseteq\mathcal P_N(F)$ with $1\leq k\leq B$, $$\dim_F\left(\sum_{i=0}^n T_iU\right)>(w-1)k. \label{eq:small-expansion}$$
  - Explanation: The sum of the images of any small subspace under the rectangular test maps has dimension strictly above the threshold needed to exclude a shrunk subspace.
- **claim-division-lift** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-division-lift)): Let $d\geq 1$, let $K$ be a field of characteristic zero, and let $\omega\in K$ be a primitive $d$th root of unity. On the standard basis $e_0,\ldots,e_{d-1}$ of $K^d$, with subscripts taken modulo $d$, set $$De_j=\omega^j e_j, \qquad Se_j=e_{j+1}.$$ For algebraically independent indeterminates $p,r$ over $K$, there is a division algebra $$\mathcal D\subseteq\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_d\bigl(K(p,r)\bigr)$$ containing, for every $R\in\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_d(K)$, the matrix $$\label{eq:division-lift} R^*=\sum_{a,b=0}^{d-1}c_{ab}p^a r^bD^aS^b, \qquad c_{ab}=\frac1d\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}\omega^{-aj}R_{j,j-b}.$$ The matrix $R^*$ specializes to $R$ at $p=r=1$. Moreover, $\mathcal D$ has dimension $d^2$ over the central subfield $K(p^d,r^d)$, and every matrix with blocks in $\mathcal D$ has rank over $K(p,r)$ divisible by $d$.
  - Explanation: Matrices over a characteristic-zero field are lifted into an explicit division algebra of matrices. Block matrices over it have rank divisible by the block size, and the lift specializes back to the original matrix.
- **claim-pencil-deformation** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-pencil-deformation)): Fix a primitive $M$th root of unity $\omega\in\mathbb C$, and lift the padded matrices $R_0(t),\ldots,R_n(t)$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:division-lift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:division-lift">6</a> with $d=M$ and $K=\mathbb Q(\omega)(t)$. Let $$L(x)=A_0+\sum_{i=1}^n A_i x_i, \qquad A_i\in\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_q(\mathbb Q),\qquad 1\leq q\leq B,$$ be an affine pencil admitting an invertible matrix evaluation over some extension field of $\mathbb Q$. Then $$\label{eq:deformed-pencil-determinant} P_{L,\omega}(t,p,r) :=\det\!\left(\sum_{i=0}^n A_i\otimes R_i^*(t,p,r)\right) \ne0.$$ This is a polynomial in $\mathbb Q(\omega)[t,p,r]$ of total degree at most $3BM^2$. In particular $\det R_0^*$ is a nonzero polynomial.
  - Explanation: For a feasible bounded-order pencil, the lifted determinant is a nonzero polynomial of bounded total degree. In particular the lifted constant-term matrix has nonzero determinant.
- **claim-invertible-witness** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-invertible-witness)): Every admissible nonzero rational formula over $\mathbb Q$ has a defined evaluation at matrices over an extension field of $\mathbb Q$ for which its value is invertible.
  - Explanation: A defined nonzero evaluation can be lifted to a defined evaluation over an extension field whose value is invertible, with no bound on the witness dimension.
- **claim-formula-realization** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-formula-realization)): For each subtree $h$ there are an affine pencil $L_h$ of order $q_h$, a constant rational row $u_h$ of length $q_h$, and a constant rational column $v_h$ of length $q_h$, such that $$q_h=2\lambda(h)+\iota(h)=\|h\|+1. \label{eq:realization-order}$$ At every matrix tuple over every field extension of $\mathbb Q$ where the original subtree $h$ is defined, $L_h$ is invertible and $$h=u_hL_h^{-1}v_h. \label{eq:realization-invariant}$$
  - Explanation: Each subtree is realized as a row-pencil-column expression whose pencil is affine, whose order is the subtree size plus one, and which is invertible wherever the subtree is defined.
- **claim-formula-certificates** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-formula-certificates)): Let $\Phi$ be a formula of size at most $s$, and put $B=2s+1$. Form the indexed collection $\mathcal C_\Phi$ consisting of $L_h$ for every inverse-node occurrence $h$ of $\Phi$, together with $J_\Phi$. Then:* 1. *The collection contains at most $s+1$ affine pencils over $\mathbb Q$, each of positive order at most $B$.* 2. *At every tuple of matrices of a common positive order over a field extension of $\mathbb Q$, all pencils in $\mathcal C_\Phi$ are invertible if and only if the original formula tree $\Phi$ is defined there and its value is invertible.* 3. *If $\Phi$ is admissible and nonzero, there is a single tuple over some field extension of $\mathbb Q$ at which every pencil in $\mathcal C_\Phi$ is invertible.* *Adding the order-one constant pencil $1$ for normalization gives at most $s+2$ pencils, still of order at most $B$, with the same simultaneous feasibility property in the third assertion.
  - Explanation: A small family of bounded-order pencils, one per inverse occurrence plus a root augmentation, is simultaneously invertible exactly when the original tree is defined with invertible value. A nonzero admissible formula admits a single tuple making all of them invertible.
- **claim-generator-hitting** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-generator-hitting)): Every nonzero admissible formula of size at most $s$ has a defined, invertible evaluation at a tuple in $\mathcal H_{n,s}$.
  - Explanation: The explicitly generated list contains, for every nonzero admissible formula of bounded size, a tuple at which the formula is defined with invertible value.
- **claim-pencil-hitting** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-pencil-hitting)): Let $L=A_0+\sum_{i=1}^n A_i x_i$ be a rational affine linear pencil of order $q\le B$ that admits an invertible matrix evaluation over an extension field of $\mathbb Q$. There is a tuple $X\in\mathcal H_{n,s}$ for which $L(X)$ is invertible.
  - Explanation: The same list hits every feasible rational affine pencil of bounded order with an invertible evaluation.
- **claim-generator-complexity** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-generator-complexity)): The list $\mathcal H_{n,s}$ can be constructed and written by one deterministic Turing machine in a number of bit operations bounded by a fixed polynomial in $n+s+1$. Its entire binary encoding has length bounded by a fixed polynomial in the same quantity.
  - Explanation: One deterministic Turing machine constructs and writes the list with bit-operation count and encoding length both bounded by fixed polynomials in the size parameter.
- **claim-rank-detecting-list** (classification, [source](#stmt-cor-rank-detecting-list)): Let $a,b\geq1$ and $k\geq0$, and put $P=a+b+k+1$. From these parameters in unary, a deterministic generator constructs a nonempty finite list $\mathcal R_{a,b,k}$ of pairs $$(e,X),\qquad e\geq1,\quad X=(X_1,\ldots,X_k)\in\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_e(\mathbb Q)^k,$$ in a fixed polynomial in $P$ bit operations. Its number of entries, total binary encoding length, and every matrix dimension $e$ are bounded by fixed polynomials in $P$. The list depends only on $a,b,k$.* *For every rectangular rational affine pencil $$A(x)=A_0+\sum_{i=1}^k A_i x_i,\qquad A_i\in\mathbb Q^{a\times b},$$ write $\operatorname{ncrk}A$ for its rank over the free skew field on $x_1,\ldots,x_k$ over $\mathbb Q$, with ordinary rational rank when $k=0$. Then the exact identity $$\operatorname{ncrk}A =\max_{(e,X)\in\mathcal R_{a,b,k}} \frac{\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}_{\mathbb Q}\!\left(A_0\otimes I_e+ \sum_{i=1}^k A_i\otimes X_i\right)}{e} \label{eq:rank-detecting-list}$$ holds for all choices and bit lengths of the coefficients.* *Suppose that, at each requested pair $(e,X)$, an oracle returns either the complete exact rational $ae$ by $be$ matrix in <a href="#eq:rank-detecting-list" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-detecting-list">[eq:rank-detecting-list]</a>, in binary rational encoding, or its exact ordinary rank. Querying the whole list nonadaptively and taking the maximum of the exact quotients returns $\operatorname{ncrk}A$. If $L$ is the total binary length of the responses, list generation, query writing, and response processing use $\operatorname{poly}(P+L)$ bit operations, in addition to the sum of the oracle’s costs. The response lengths and oracle costs may depend on the coefficient heights. If $a=0$ or $b=0$, the rank is zero and no queries are needed.
  - Explanation: A coefficient-independent nonadaptive list recovers the noncommutative rank of every rectangular rational affine pencil as a maximum of normalized evaluation ranks, with accounted processing cost.

      "explanation": "If a bounded-order pencil has an invertible evaluation over some extension field, its evaluation on the rectangular test operators is injective. The padded square version then has rank strictly above that of a deficient block.",

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-division-lift",
        "exact_quote": "Let $d\\geq 1$, let $K$ be a field of characteristic zero, and let $\\omega\\in K$ be a primitive $d$th root of unity. On the standard basis $e_0,\\ldots,e_{d-1}$ of $K^d$, with subscripts taken modulo $d$, set $$De_j=\\omega^j e_j,\n  \\qquad Se_j=e_{j+1}.$$ For algebraically independent indeterminates $p,r$ over $K$, there is a division algebra $$\\mathcal D\\subseteq\\mathop{\\mathrm{Mat}}_d\\bigl(K(p,r)\\bigr)$$ containing, for every $R\\in\\mathop{\\mathrm{Mat}}_d(K)$, the matrix $$\\label{eq:division-lift}\n  R^*=\\sum_{a,b=0}^{d-1}c_{ab}p^a r^bD^aS^b,\n  \\qquad\n  c_{ab}=\\frac1d\\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}\\omega^{-aj}R_{j,j-b}.$$ The matrix $R^*$ specializes to $R$ at $p=r=1$. Moreover, $\\mathcal D$ has dimension $d^2$ over the central subfield $K(p^d,r^d)$, and every matrix with blocks in $\\mathcal D$ has rank over $K(p,r)$ divisible by $d$.",

      },
      "evidence": [

        "exact_quote": "Fix a primitive $M$th root of unity $\\omega\\in\\mathbb C$, and lift the padded matrices $R_0(t),\\ldots,R_n(t)$ by Lemma <a href=\"#lem:division-lift\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:division-lift\">6</a> with $d=M$ and $K=\\mathbb Q(\\omega)(t)$. Let $$L(x)=A_0+\\sum_{i=1}^n A_i x_i,\n  \\qquad A_i\\in\\mathop{\\mathrm{Mat}}_q(\\mathbb Q),\\qquad 1\\leq q\\leq B,$$ be an affine pencil admitting an invertible matrix evaluation over some extension field of $\\mathbb Q$. Then $$\\label{eq:deformed-pencil-determinant}\n  P_{L,\\omega}(t,p,r)\n   :=\\det\\!\\left(\\sum_{i=0}^n A_i\\otimes R_i^*(t,p,r)\\right)\n   \\ne0.$$ This is a polynomial in $\\mathbb Q(\\omega)[t,p,r]$ of total degree at most $3BM^2$.         "anchor": "stmt-lem-formula-realization",
        "exact_quote": "For each subtree $h$ there are an affine pencil $L_h$ of order $q_h$, a constant rational row $u_h$ of length $q_h$, and a constant rational column $v_h$ of length $q_h$, such that $$q_h=2\\lambda(h)+\\iota(h)=|h|+1.\n \\label{eq:realization-order}$$ At every matrix tuple over every field extension of $\\mathbb Q$ where the original subtree $h$ is defined, $L_h$ is invertible and $$h=u_hL_h^{-1}v_h.\n \\label{eq:realization-invariant}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-116.3"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-formula-certificates",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\Phi$ be a formula of size at most $s$, and put $B=2s+1$. Form the indexed collection $\\mathcal C_\\Phi$ consisting of $L_h$ for every inverse-node occurrence $h$ of $\\Phi$, together with $J_\\Phi$. Then:*\n\n1.  *The collection contains at most $s+1$ affine pencils over $\\mathbb Q$, each of positive order at most $B$.*\n\n2.  *At every tuple of matrices of a common positive order over a field extension of $\\mathbb Q$, all pencils in $\\mathcal C_\\Phi$ are invertible if and only if the original formula tree $\\Phi$ is defined there and its value is invertible.*\n\n3.  *If $\\Phi$ is admissible and nonzero, there is a single tuple over some field extension of $\\mathbb Q$ at which every pencil in $\\mathcal C_\\Phi$ is invertible.*\n\n*Adding the order-one constant pencil $1$ for normalization gives at most $s+2$ pencils, still of order at most $B$, with the same simultaneous feasibility property in the third assertion.",
        "source_id": "PM-116.3"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-prop-formula-certificates",
          "exact_quote": "Let $\\Phi$ be a formula of size at most $s$, and put $B=2s+1$. Form the indexed collection $\\mathcal C_\\Phi$ consisting of $L_h$ for every inverse-node occurrence $h$ of $\\Phi$, together with $J_\\Phi$. Then:*\n\n1.  *The collection contains at most $s+1$ affine pencils over $\\mathbb Q$, each of positive order at most $B$.*\n\n2.  *At every tuple of matrices of a common positive order over a field extension of $\\mathbb Q$, all pencils in $\\mathcal C_\\Phi$ are invertible if and only if the original formula tree $\\Phi$ is defined there and its value is invertible.*\n\n3.  *If $\\Phi$ is admissible and nonzero, there is a single tuple over some field extension of $\\mathbb Q$ at which every pencil in $\\mathcal C_\\Phi$ is invertible.*\n\n*Adding the order-one constant pencil $1$ for normalization gives at most $s+2$ pencils, still of order at most $B$, with the same simultaneous feasibility property in the third assertion.",
          "source_id": "PM-116.3"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $\\Phi$ be a formula of size at most $s$, and put $B=2s+1$. Form the indexed collection $\\mathcal C_\\Phi$ consisting of $L_h$ for every inverse-node occurrence $h$ of $\\Phi$, together with $J_\\Phi$. Then:*\n\n1.  *The collection contains at most $s+1$ affine pencils over $\\mathbb Q$, each of positive order at most $B$.*\n\n2.  *At every tuple of matrices of a common positive order over a field extension of $\\mathbb Q$, all pencils in $\\mathcal C_\\Phi$ are invertible if and only if the original formula tree $\\Phi$ is defined there and its value is invertible.*\n\n3.  *If $\\Phi$ is admissible and nonzero, there is a single tuple over some field extension of $\\mathbb Q$ at which every pencil in $\\mathcal C_\\Phi$ is invertible.*\n\n*Adding the order-one constant pencil $1$ for normalization gives at most $s+2$ pencils, still of order at most $B$, with the same simultaneous feasibility property in the third assertion.",
      "explanation": "A small family of bounded-order pencils, one per inverse occurrence plus a root augmentation, is simultaneously invertible exactly when the original tree is defined with invertible value. A nonzero admissible formula admits a single tuple making all of them invertible.",

        "exact_quote": "Let $a,b\\geq1$ and $k\\geq0$, and put $P=a+b+k+1$. From these parameters in unary, a deterministic generator constructs a nonempty finite list $\\mathcal R_{a,b,k}$ of pairs $$(e,X),\\qquad e\\geq1,\\quad\n X=(X_1,\\ldots,X_k)\\in\\mathop{\\mathrm{Mat}}_e(\\mathbb Q)^k,$$ in a fixed polynomial in $P$ bit operations. Its number of entries, total binary encoding length, and every matrix dimension $e$ are bounded by fixed polynomials in $P$. The list depends only on $a,b,k$.*\n\n\n*For every rectangular rational affine pencil $$A(x)=A_0+\\sum_{i=1}^k A_i x_i,\\qquad A_i\\in\\mathbb Q^{a\\times b},$$ write $\\operatorname{ncrk}A$ for its rank over the free skew field on $x_1,\\ldots,x_k$ over $\\mathbb Q$, with ordinary rational rank when $k=0$. Then the exact identity $$\\operatorname{ncrk}A\n =\\max_{(e,X)\\in\\mathcal R_{a,b,k}}\n   \\frac{\\mathop{\\mathrm{rank}}_{\\mathbb Q}\\!\\left(A_0\\otimes I_e+\n             \\sum_{i=1}^k A_i\\otimes X_i\\right)}{e}\n \\label{eq:rank-detecting-list}$$ holds for all choices and bit lengths of the coefficients.*\n\n*Suppose that, at each requested pair $(e,X)$, an oracle returns either the complete exact rational $ae$ by $be$ matrix in <a href=\"#eq:rank-detecting-list\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:rank-detecting-list\">[eq:rank-detecting-list]</a>, in binary rational encoding, or its exact ordinary rank. Querying the whole list nonadaptively and taking the maximum of the exact quotients returns $\\operatorname{ncrk}A$. If $L$ is the total binary length of the responses, list generation, query writing, and response processing use $\\operatorname{poly}(P+L)$ bit operations, in addition to the sum of the oracle’s costs. The response lengths and oracle costs may depend on the coefficient heights. If $a=0$ or $b=0$, the rank is zero and no queries are needed.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **noncommutative rational formula** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A noncommutative rational formula is a rooted tree whose leaves are variables or rational constants, whose binary gates are addition or ordered multiplication, and whose unary gates are inversion.
- **formula size** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Its size is its number of vertices, including leaves and inverse gates.
- **admissible and nonzero formula** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Call a formula *admissible* if it has a defined rational matrix evaluation of some positive dimension, and *nonzero* if one of its defined rational matrix evaluations is nonzero.
- **matrix hitting list** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A matrix hitting list is a fixed finite list of tuples that supplies a defined nonzero evaluation for each nonzero formula in the prescribed class.
- **feasible pencil** ([source](#pm-h-4)): Call the pencil *feasible* if one such evaluation is invertible.
- **noncommutative rank** ([source](#pm-h-4)): Their *noncommutative rank* is their rank over the free skew field.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-expansion-points | number of expansion points w | = | $n+1$ | n variables |
| q-pencil-order-bound | pencil order bound B | = | $2s+1$ | formula size s |
| q-padding-width | padding width Delta | = | $2w^3B$ | w and B as defined |
| q-input-dimension | input polynomial dimension N | = | $M-\Delta$ | M and Delta as defined |
| q-block-dimension-bound | padded block size M | ≤ | $2B\Delta$ | M a power of two exceeding B times Delta |
| q-wronskian-order-sum | total vanishing order over all points | ≤ | $h(d-1)+w\binom{h}{2}$ | dimension h, degree bound d, w points |
| q-padded-rank | rank of padded pencil evaluation over the rational function field | > | $(q-1)M$ | q, N, M as defined |
| q-determinant-degree | total degree of lifted determinant polynomial | ≤ | $3BM^2$ | order q at most B |
| q-subtree-pencil-order | order of subtree pencil | = | $\|h\|+1$ | subtree h with leaf and inverse counts |
| q-list-matrix-dimension | common matrix dimension D of the output tuples | = | $\frac{M^2}{2}$ | M a power of two |
| q-grid-size | grid side length H | = | $1+3(s+2)BM^3$ | s, B, M as defined |
| q-dimension-asymptotic | matrix dimension in terms of m | = | $O(m^{10})$ | m=n+s+1 |
| t-generator-bit-operations | bit operations of the generator | ≤ | $C(n+s+1)^k$ | fixed positive integers C and k |
| s-output-bits | total output length in bits | ≤ | $C(n+s+1)^k$ | fixed positive integers C and k |
| s-output-bits-explicit | explicit encoding length of the list | = | $O(m^{87}\log m)$ | m=n+s+1 |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-wronskian** [claim](#stmt-lem-wronskian-orders) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-wronskian-orders): Leading-term Vandermonde computation of the Wronskian at each point, compared with its degree. -> bound on the total vanishing orders
- **step-integral** [claim](#stmt-lem-integral-reduction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-integral-reduction): Induction producing an integral basis with independent reductions via a unit coordinate. -> integral basis and reduced space of full dimension
- **step-expansion** [claim](#stmt-lem-small-expansion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-small-expansion): Taylor reversal of leading reductions combined with the Wronskian bound, then contradiction. -> strict dimension expansion of small subspaces
- **step-rectangular** [claim](#stmt-prop-rectangular) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-small-expansion-2): Shrunk-subspace obstruction from a kernel vector, transferred to the witness over the extension field. -> injectivity and padded rank bound
- **step-division-lift** [claim](#stmt-lem-division-lift) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-division-lift): Fourier inversion, ordered monomials with twisted commutation, finite-dimensional division algebra, block elimination. -> division algebra lift with rank divisibility
- **step-deformation** [claim](#stmt-prop-pencil-deformation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-pencil-deformation): Lift a nonzero minor, use rank divisibility to force full rank, bound the degree entrywise. -> nonzero determinant polynomial of bounded degree
- **step-witness** [claim](#stmt-lem-invertible-witness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-invertible-witness): Lift a nonzero evaluation, verify the domain gate by gate over a local ring, conclude invertibility in the division algebra. -> invertible witness over an extension field
- **step-realization** [claim](#stmt-lem-formula-realization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-formula-realization): Gate-by-gate block constructions with Schur complement factorization at inverse gates. -> affine pencil with row and column selectors for each subtree
- **step-certificates** [claim](#stmt-prop-formula-certificates) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-formula-certificates): Induction from leaves to root on definedness using pencil invertibility; existence from the invertible witness. -> simultaneous invertibility criterion for the formula domain
- **step-generator-hitting** [claim](#stmt-prop-generator-hitting) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-generator-hitting): Product of determinant polynomials over roots of unity and pencils, nonvanishing at a grid point, rational splitting. -> hitting tuple in the list
- **step-pencil-hitting** [claim](#stmt-cor-pencil-hitting) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-pencil-hitting): Same argument with the pencil and the constant pencil. -> invertible evaluation of a feasible pencil in the list
- **step-complexity** [claim](#stmt-prop-generator-complexity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-generator-complexity): Parameter bounds, integer size bounds, Cramer's rule and fraction-controlled Gaussian elimination. -> polynomial bit time and output length
- **step-main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-generator-complexity-2): Combine the hitting property with the complexity bound. -> main theorem
- **step-rank** [claim](#stmt-cor-rank-detecting-list) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-rank-detecting-list): Square completions as pencils in a fixed variable pool, then the pencil hitting list projected to the first variables. -> rank-detecting list

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-subtree-realization**: Each subtree of a formula is realized by an affine pencil with constant selector row and column. The pencil order is the subtree size plus one. Wherever the subtree is defined, the pencil is invertible and the selectors return the subtree value.
- **inv-certificate-equivalence**: For a formula of bounded size, the inverse-node pencils together with the root augmentation are all invertible at a tuple exactly when the original tree is defined there and its value is invertible.
- **inv-inner-inverse-retained**: A formula that simplifies to a function defined at a point can still be undefined there; the inner inverse pencil is what rejects such a point.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-drop-inner-inverse**: Check only the outermost inverse pencil and the root augmentation to decide whether a formula is defined at a tuple. -> A tuple at which an inner inverse gate meets a singular operand is wrongly accepted, because later cancellation hides the singularity.
- **anti-simplify-before-testing**: Simplify a formula algebraically and then test the simplified expression on the hitting list. -> The simplified expression may have a larger evaluation domain than the original tree, so the guarantee about defined invertible values no longer refers to the formula actually given.
- **anti-harness-as-generator**: Treat the pencil-building harness, which reads a formula, as a stand-in for the hitting-list generator. -> The harness inspects a formula and a tuple, so it says nothing about a universal list that is produced without any formula.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The main generator has a very large output dimension and is not run. The finite explicit part is the affine realization of a formula tree and the certificate pencils; it is built and tested exactly at small rational matrix tuples against direct tree evaluation.

```python
# INPUTS: one JSON object on stdin: d (matrix size), X (list of square rational matrices), formula (nested list tree).
# PRECONDITIONS: variables indexed from 1; entries are integers or fraction strings; gates var, const, add, mul, inv.
# OUTPUTS: lines 'q <order>', 'defined <0|1>', and when defined a line 'value' followed by d rows of u L^-1 v.
# TIME: polynomial in tree size and d with exact rational arithmetic.
# SPACE: polynomial in tree size and d.
# ARITHMETIC: exact Fractions only.
# FAILURE: an unknown gate raises an error; a singular certificate pencil gives defined 0.
import sys, json
from fractions import Fraction as Fr

def zp(q, n):
    return [[[Fr(0)]*q for _ in range(q)] for _ in range(n+1)]

def aug(A, u, v, n):
    qg = len(u)
    P = zp(qg+1, n)
    for k in range(n+1):
        for a in range(qg):
            for b in range(qg):
                P[k][a][b] = A[k][a][b]
    for a in range(qg):
        P[0][a][qg] = v[a]
        P[0][qg][a] = u[a]
    return P

def build(f, n, invs):
    t = f[0]
    if t in ('var', 'const'):
        A = zp(2, n)
        A[0][0][0] = Fr(1)
        A[0][1][1] = Fr(1)
        if t == 'var':
            A[f[1]][0][1] = Fr(-1)
        else:
            A[0][0][1] = -Fr(str(f[1]))
        return A, [Fr(1), Fr(0)], [Fr(0), Fr(1)]
    if t == 'inv':
        Ag, ug, vg = build(f[1], n, invs)
        qg = len(ug)
        P = aug(Ag, ug, vg, n)
        invs.append(P)
        return P, [Fr(0)]*qg+[Fr(-1)], [Fr(0)]*qg+[Fr(1)]
    Af, uf, vf = build(f[1], n, invs)
    Ag, ug, vg = build(f[2], n, invs)
    qf = len(uf)
    qg = len(ug)
    A = zp(qf+qg, n)
    for k in range(n+1):
        for a in range(qf):
            for b in range(qf):
                A[k][a][b] = Af[k][a][b]
        for a in range(qg):
            for b in range(qg):
                A[k][qf+a][qf+b] = Ag[k][a][b]
    if t == 'add':
        return A, uf+ug, vf+vg
    if t != 'mul':
        raise ValueError('gate')
    for a in range(qf):
        for b in range(qg):
            A[0][a][qf+b] = -vf[a]*ug[b]
    return A, uf+[Fr(0)]*qg, [Fr(0)]*qf+vg

def evalL(A, X, d):
    q = len(A[0])
    M = [[Fr(0)]*(q*d) for _ in range(q*d)]
    for a in range(q):
        for b in range(q):
            for i in range(d):
                M[a*d+i][b*d+i] += A[0][a][b]
    for k in range(1, len(A)):
        Xk = X[k-1]
        for a in range(q):
            for b in range(q):
                for i in range(d):
                    for j in range(d):
                        M[a*d+i][b*d+j] += A[k][a][b]*Xk[i][j]
    return M

def solve(M, B):
    n = len(M)
    G = [M[r][:] + B[r][:] for r in range(n)]
    for c in range(n):
        p = None
        for r in range(c, n):
            if G[r][c] != 0:
                p = r
                break
        if p is None:
            return None
        G[c], G[p] = G[p], G[c]
        pv = G[c][c]
        G[c] = [x/pv for x in G[c]]
        for r in range(n):
            if r != c and G[r][c] != 0:
                fct = G[r][c]
                G[r] = [x-fct*y for x, y in zip(G[r], G[c])]
    return [row[n:] for row in G]

def inv_ok(P, X, d):
    M = evalL(P, X, d)
    N = len(M)
    I = [[Fr(1) if i == j else Fr(0) for j in range(N)] for i in range(N)]
    return solve(M, I) is not None

def main():
    inp = json.loads(sys.stdin.read())
    d = inp['d']
    X = [[[Fr(str(e)) for e in row] for row in m] for m in inp['X']]
    n = len(X)
    invs = []
    A, u, v = build(inp['formula'], n, invs)
    q = len(u)
    J = aug(A, u, v, n)
    ok = all(inv_ok(P, X, d) for P in invs) and inv_ok(J, X, d)
    val = None
    if ok:
        B = [[v[a] if i == j else Fr(0) for j in range(d)] for a in range(q) for i in range(d)]
        S = solve(evalL(A, X, d), B)
        if S is not None:
            val = [[sum(u[a]*S[a*d+i][j] for a in range(q)) for j in range(d)] for i in range(d)]
    print('q', q)
    if val is None:
        print('defined 0')
    else:
        print('defined 1')
        print('value')
        for row in val:
            print(' '.join(str(x) for x in row))

main()
```

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | A black-box identity tester for rational formulas can be built by evaluating an unknown formula at each tuple of the precomputed list. The list is a fixed, formula-independent test fixture. The unknown formula's evaluation cost is outside the generator bound. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper is exact algebra over rational matrices with no dynamics, state evolution or real-time behaviour that a simulation or game could use. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No standalone tool or interface is specified in the paper. Tooling would only be a wrapper around the generator, which is covered under software and algorithms. |
| algorithms | True | strong | implementable | inferred | The generator is an explicit finite procedure: it computes the parameters, builds the lifted matrices at every grid triple, discards singular ones, and emits the normalized tuples. It is deterministic, takes only unary inputs, and has a stated polynomial bit-time bound. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper gives no data structure with query or update operations. Matrices and records only serve as storage inside the generator. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper states no hardware-oriented construction. The cost model is Turing-machine bit operations, and the matrix dimension is polynomially large, which is not an accelerator design. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result is identity testing over the rationals. It has no hardness assumption, key, adversary or security notion, and the generator uses no randomness. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no learning, training or statistical estimation in the paper. All claims are exact algebraic existence statements about a deterministic list. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No objective function is optimised. The grid search only guarantees that some good triple exists in a fixed set, with no cost minimisation or feasibility programme. |
| distributed_systems | True | weak | inspirational | inferred | The rank-detecting list is queried nonadaptively, so all evaluation requests can be issued up front to independent workers and the responses combined. This is an inferred use, not a stated distributed protocol. |
| other (computer algebra and noncommutative identity testing) | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The main use is testing nonzeroness of noncommutative rational expressions, and computing the exact noncommutative rank of rectangular affine pencils from ordinary ranks at a coefficient-independent list. This does not fit the other ten domains. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-admissible-nonzero** (hypothesis): The hitting guarantee holds only for formulas that are admissible and nonzero in the operational sense: some defined rational matrix evaluation exists, and one defined evaluation is nonzero. Algebraic cancellation does not enlarge the domain. Blocked use: ['Applying the list to a formula with an empty evaluation domain and expecting a defined hit', 'Replacing the given tree by a simplified expression and expecting the same guarantee']
- **limit-size-and-variable-bound** (hypothesis): The list is built from the unary bounds on the variable count and the formula size, so it serves only formulas in the stated number of variables whose size does not exceed the bound. Blocked use: ['Reusing a list for a formula with more variables than n', 'Reusing a list for a formula larger than s']
- **limit-evaluation-cost-excluded** (edge_case): The polynomial bound concerns generating the list only. The cost of evaluating an unknown formula with arbitrarily large rational constants is not included. Blocked use: ['Claiming a polynomial total identity-testing time independent of constant bit lengths']
- **limit-characteristic-zero-lift** (hypothesis): The division algebra lift is stated for a field of characteristic zero containing a primitive root of unity; the construction is established over the rationals only. Blocked use: ['Running the generator or the lift over finite fields', 'Assuming the same list works in positive characteristic']
- **limit-witness-existential** (nonconstructive): The invertible witness, the subspaces and the formula-specific determinant product are existence tools. None is computed by the generator, and the witness dimension is not bounded. Blocked use: ['Using the witness dimension as a resource bound', 'Trying to compute the good grid triple for a formula without evaluating it']
- **limit-rank-oracle-model** (formal_scope): The rank-detecting list assumes an oracle returning exact matrix values or exact ordinary ranks. It makes no claim for arbitrary rational circuits, finite fields, or oracle cost independent of coefficient heights. Blocked use: ['Using the list with approximate numerical rank responses', 'Claiming oracle cost independent of coefficient heights']
- **limit-large-output** (approximation): The polynomial bounds have large exponents and the common matrix dimension is a high-degree polynomial, so the list is a complexity-theoretic object rather than a practical test fixture. Blocked use: ['Materializing the list for realistic parameters in production testing']

>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalization gives one explicit tuple of rational matrices that simultaneously detects every nonzero division-free noncommutative formula with at most $s$ gates in $n$ variables, for $n,s\ge1$. Evaluation at that tuple is a nonzero matrix over every characteristic-zero field.
>
> The selected theorem states this universal hitting property. The paper's deterministic polynomial bit-construction bound and matrix-dimension bound $O(ns^2)$ are not separately asserted in it.
>
> The formalized result constructs polynomial-size hitting lists for noncommutative rational formulas with rational constants, addition, multiplication, and inverse gates. Given the number of variables and a formula-size bound, one deterministic polynomial-time machine outputs rational matrix tuples of a common positive dimension. Every admissible nonzero formula within the size bound evaluates to an invertible matrix on some listed tuple. The matrix dimension, complete binary output length, and running time are all polynomially bounded.

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace RationalHitting

universe u

inductive Formula (n : ℕ) where
  | var : Fin n → Formula n
  | const : ℚ → Formula n
  | add : Formula n → Formula n → Formula n
  | mul : Formula n → Formula n → Formula n
  | inv : Formula n → Formula n
  deriving DecidableEq

namespace Formula

def size {n : ℕ} : Formula n → ℕ
  | .var _ | .const _ => 1
  | .add f g | .mul f g => f.size + g.size + 1
  | .inv f => f.size + 1

/-- Evaluation in any rational algebra; a unary inverse requires an actual
    two-sided inverse of the evaluated operand, not a totalized inverse. -/
inductive Eval {n : ℕ} {R : Type u} [Ring R]
    (c : ℚ →+* R) (X : Fin n → R) : Formula n → R → Prop where
  | var (i) : Eval c X (.var i) (X i)
  | const (a) : Eval c X (.const a) (c a)
  | add {f g a b} : Eval c X f a → Eval c X g b → Eval c X (.add f g) (a + b)
  | mul {f g a b} : Eval c X f a → Eval c X g b → Eval c X (.mul f g) (a * b)
  | inv {f a b} : Eval c X f a → a * b = 1 → b * a = 1 → Eval c X (.inv f) b

end Formula

abbrev Mat (d : ℕ) := Matrix (Fin d) (Fin d) ℚ
abbrev Tuple (n d : ℕ) := Fin n → Mat d

def Evaluates {n d : ℕ} (f : Formula n) (X : Tuple n d) (v : Mat d) : Prop :=
  f.Eval (algebraMap ℚ (Mat d)) X v

def Admissible {n : ℕ} (f : Formula n) : Prop :=
  ∃ d, 0 < d ∧ ∃ (X : Tuple n d), ∃ v, Evaluates f X v

def Nonzero {n : ℕ} (f : Formula n) : Prop :=
  ∃ d, 0 < d ∧ ∃ (X : Tuple n d), ∃ v, Evaluates f X v ∧ v ≠ 0

/-- All output tuples have one common positive dimension. -/
structure Output (n : ℕ) where
  dimension : ℕ
  dimension_pos : 0 < dimension
  tuples : List (Tuple n dimension)

/-- Little-endian unsigned binary, prefixed by a unary bit-length and a zero
    terminator. This is a self-delimiting binary encoding, including zero. -/
def encodeNat (a : ℕ) : List Bool :=
  let ds := (Nat.digits 2 a).map (fun b => b != 0)
  List.replicate ds.length true ++ [false] ++ ds

/-- Sign bit, binary numerator magnitude, and positive binary denominator.
    Rat.num and Rat.den are the canonical reduced numerator and denominator. -/
def encodeRat (a : ℚ) : List Bool :=
  [decide (a.num < 0)] ++ encodeNat a.num.natAbs ++ encodeNat a.den

def encodeTuple {n d : ℕ} (X : Tuple n d) : List Bool :=
  (List.finRange n).flatMap fun i =>
    (List.finRange d).flatMap fun j =>
      (List.finRange d).flatMap fun k => encodeRat (X i j k)

def encodeOutput {n : ℕ} (H : Output n) : List Bool :=
  encodeNat H.dimension ++ encodeNat H.tuples.length ++ H.tuples.flatMap encodeTuple

/-- 0 is blank, 1 and 2 are the two literal binary digits, 3 is work space.
    Fixed finite tape alphabet and finite state space; no advice or oracle. -/
abbrev Alphabet := Fin 4
abbrev Machine (m : ℕ) := Turing.TM0.Machine Alphabet (Fin (m + 1))
abbrev Config (m : ℕ) := Turing.TM0.Cfg Alphabet (Fin (m + 1))

def tapeBit (b : Bool) : Alphabet := if b then 2 else 1

def unaryInput (n s : ℕ) : List Alphabet :=
  List.replicate n 2 ++ [1] ++ List.replicate s 2

/-- Exactly t ordinary deterministic TM0 transitions. -/
def run {m : ℕ} (G : Machine m) : ℕ → Config m → Option (Config m)
  | 0, a => some a
  | t + 1, a => (Turing.TM0.step G a).bind (run G t)

def OutputsWithin {m n : ℕ} (G : Machine m) (s bound : ℕ) (H : Output n) : Prop :=
  ∃ t ≤ bound, ∃ a : Config m,
    run G t (Turing.TM0.init (unaryInput n s)) = some a ∧
    Turing.TM0.step G a = none ∧
    a.Tape.right₀ = Turing.ListBlank.mk ((encodeOutput H).map tapeBit)

def Hits {n : ℕ} (s : ℕ) (H : Output n) : Prop :=
  ∀ f : Formula n, f.size ≤ s → Admissible f → Nonzero f →
    ∃ X ∈ H.tuples, ∃ v, Evaluates f X v ∧ IsUnit v

/-- A fixed polynomial bound on bit time, full binary output length,
and common positive dimension. -/
def MainStatement : Prop :=
  ∃ (m : ℕ) (G : Machine m) (C k : ℕ), 0 < C ∧ 0 < k ∧
    ∀ n s : ℕ, 1 ≤ n → 1 ≤ s → ∃ H : Output n,
      H.dimension ≤ C * (n + s + 1) ^ k ∧
      (encodeOutput H).length ≤ C * (n + s + 1) ^ k ∧
      OutputsWithin G s (C * (n + s + 1) ^ k) H ∧ Hits s H

theorem main : MainStatement := by
  sorry

end RationalHitting

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We construct, in deterministic polynomial bit time, a polynomial-size list of rational matrix tuples for noncommutative rational formulas over ℚ of bounded tree size. Every nonzero admissible formula has a defined, invertible value at one tuple, with no separate bounds on inverse nesting or rational constant heights. Matrix dimensions, entry bit lengths, and total output length are polynomially bounded.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

A noncommutative rational formula is a rooted tree whose leaves are variables or rational constants, whose binary gates are addition or ordered multiplication, and whose unary gates are inversion. Its size is its number of vertices, including leaves and inverse gates. At a tuple of square rational matrices, a scalar is interpreted as a scalar multiple of the identity. Evaluation is defined only when every inverse gate encounters an invertible matrix.

Call a formula *admissible* if it has a defined rational matrix evaluation of some positive dimension, and *nonzero* if one of its defined rational matrix evaluations is nonzero. These operational definitions suffice for the hitting-list theorem; its proof does not need a construction of the free skew field. In particular, algebraic cancellations do not enlarge a formula’s domain of evaluation.

Identity testing asks whether an admissible formula is zero at every defined evaluation. Scalar substitutions cannot answer this question: for example, $x_1x_2-x_2x_1$ vanishes on all scalar tuples but is nonzero on matrices. A matrix hitting list is a fixed finite list of tuples that supplies a defined nonzero evaluation for each nonzero formula in the prescribed class. Such a list permits testing through evaluations without access to the formula tree. With inverse gates, the same tuple must satisfy every domain condition imposed by that tree, including conditions in branches that later cancel. In oracle terminology, an undefined evaluation is reported as such; only defined outputs are tested for zero.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There are a deterministic Turing machine $G$ and fixed positive integers $C,k$ with the following property. Given $1^n,1^s$, where $n,s\geq1$, the machine outputs a finite list $\mathcal H_{n,s}$ of $n$-tuples of rational square matrices, in at most $C(n+s+1)^k$ bit operations. The entire output, with signed binary numerators and positive binary denominators, has at most $C(n+s+1)^k$ bits. For every nonzero admissible rational formula $\Phi$ in $n$ variables of size at most $s$, some tuple in $\mathcal H_{n,s}$ is a defined evaluation of $\Phi$ with invertible value.*

*The same list works for all such formulas, regardless of the bit lengths of their rational constants or their inverse depth. The generator receives no formula, oracle, randomness, or advice.*

</div>

The invertible-value conclusion is stronger than the nonzero-value requirement. All tuples produced below even have one common dimension depending only on $n,s$.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Context

The algebraic setting comes from the theory of rational identities and universal fields of fractions (Amitsur 1966; Cohn 2006) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Amitsur66](#ref-Amitsur66), [Cohn06](#ref-Cohn06)]. Affine-matrix representations of free-field elements appear in Cohn and Reutenauer (Cohn and Reutenauer 1994, sec. 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[CR94](#ref-CR94)]; Kaliuzhnyi-Verbovetskyi and Vinnikov give a systematic account of matrix evaluation domains and rational functions (Kaliuzhnyi-Verbovetskyi and Vinnikov 2010, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[KVV10](#ref-KVV10)]. The distinction between a rational function and an expression representing it matters here: equivalent expressions can impose different conditions on their intermediate inverses. Our operational formulation retains the original expression throughout.

For division-free computation, Nisan’s coefficient-matrix characterization made rank a central tool in noncommutative branching-program complexity (Nisan 1991) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Nisan91](#ref-Nisan91)]. Raz and Shpilka gave deterministic polynomial-time identity testing with access to a formula or branching program (Raz and Shpilka 2005) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[RS05](#ref-RS05)]. Bogdanov and Wee developed randomized black-box matrix evaluation for bounded-degree noncommutative polynomials (Bogdanov and Wee 2005) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[BW05](#ref-BW05)], and Forbes and Shpilka constructed deterministic quasipolynomial-size matrix hitting sets for noncommutative algebraic branching programs, a model including division-free formulas (Forbes and Shpilka 2013) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[FS13](#ref-FS13)].

For computation with division, Hrubeš and Wigderson developed the rational-formula model and its reduction to linear-matrix singularity testing (Hrubeš and Wigderson 2015) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[HW15](#ref-HW15)]. Their inverse-matrix realizations and tests for the domains of gate subformulas are direct predecessors of our certificate construction. Deterministic polynomial-time algorithms with access to the input formula followed from operator scaling and constructive noncommutative-rank computation (Garg et al. 2016, 2020; Ivanyos, Qiao, and Subrahmanyam 2018) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[GGOW16](#ref-GGOW16), [GGOW20](#ref-GGOW20), [IQS18](#ref-IQS18)]. These algorithms use the given coefficients, with their encoding lengths included in the bit model. Derksen and Makam established polynomial bounds on the dimensions needed for an admissible rational formula to have a defined evaluation, and derived randomized polynomial-time rational identity testing (Derksen and Makam 2017, sec. 6) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[DM17](#ref-DM17)]. Small witnesses and algorithms that depend on the input coefficients leave open the construction of a universal deterministic list from size bounds alone.

Arvind, Chatterjee, and Mukhopadhyay obtained deterministic quasipolynomial-size hitting sets for rational formulas of inversion height two (Arvind, Chatterjee, and Mukhopadhyay 2022, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[ACM22](#ref-ACM22)]. Their subsequent general result handles unrestricted inverse nesting with quasipolynomial-size rational matrix hitting sets (Arvind, Chatterjee, and Mukhopadhyay 2026) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[ACM26](#ref-ACM26)]; the quantitative bounds used in this comparison are Theorem 2, Corollary 3, and the paragraph following Remark 52 of the specified preprint version (Arvind, Chatterjee, and Mukhopadhyay 2025) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[ACM25](#ref-ACM25)]. Thus rational matrix outputs and arbitrary inverse nesting already occur in the preceding literature. Invertible evaluations are also used in the division-algebra methods of these works (Arvind, Chatterjee, and Mukhopadhyay 2022, 2025) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[ACM22](#ref-ACM22), [ACM25](#ref-ACM25)]. Their general treatment states a depth reduction that preserves the input formula’s evaluation domain (Arvind, Chatterjee, and Mukhopadhyay 2025, Fact 1) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[ACM25](#ref-ACM25)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> gives polynomial bounds on construction time, total binary output length, and matrix dimension. Its complexity bound concerns generation of the list; the cost of evaluating an unknown formula with arbitrarily large rational constants is not included.

The result resolves, over $\mathbb Q$, the explicit polynomial-size hitting-set problem for this formula model, and gives the polynomial matrix dimension asked for in the conclusion of Arvind, Chatterjee, and Mukhopadhyay (2025) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[ACM25](#ref-ACM25)]. The proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> below establishes all the algebraic and algorithmic ingredients it uses.

The proof also draws on established methods. The multiplicity argument for explicit subspace designs of Guruswami and Kopparty (Guruswami and Kopparty 2016) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[GK16](#ref-GK16)], and the connection between subspace designs, rank condensers, and dimension expansion studied by Forbes and Guruswami (Forbes and Guruswami 2015) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[FG15](#ref-FG15)], provide context for our construction. The underlying Wronskian calculation is classical (Bostan and Dumas 2010) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[BD10](#ref-BD10)]. The common-subspace obstruction to invertibility belongs to noncommutative-rank theory (Fortin and Reutenauer 2004) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[FR04](#ref-FR04)], while cyclic division algebras and rank divisibility underlie the regularity methods of Ivanyos, Qiao, and Subrahmanyam (Ivanyos, Qiao, and Subrahmanyam 2017, 2018) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[IQS17](#ref-IQS17), [IQS18](#ref-IQS18)]. We prove the precise expansion, deformation, and domain statements needed here, without importing the stronger general algorithms from those works.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The construction and its logic

Write $\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_q(F)$ for the $q$ by $q$ matrices over a field $F$. The bridge from formula evaluation to linear algebra is an *affine linear pencil*, a matrix $$L(x)=A_0+\sum_{i=1}^n A_ix_i,\qquad A_i\in\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_q(\mathbb Q),$$ of order $q$. Its evaluation at matrices $X_i\in\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_e(E)$ over an extension field $E/\mathbb Q$ is $A_0\otimes I_e+\sum_iA_i\otimes X_i$. Call the pencil *feasible* if one such evaluation is invertible. Tensor products and ranks are taken over the indicated field.

A size-$s$ formula gives at most $s+1$ pencils of order at most $B=2s+1$. Their simultaneous invertibility certifies that every inverse occurrence in the original tree is defined and that the final value is invertible. A nonzero admissible formula has a matrix evaluation over an extension field satisfying all these conditions. Section <a href="#sec:encoding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:encoding">4</a> proves this reduction, including the exact conditions at the inverse gates. The construction therefore seeks a universal rational list on which each such finite family of feasible pencils is simultaneously invertible.

Two obstacles remain: obtaining full rank at matrices independent of the pencil coefficients, and keeping all conditions valid when the parameters become rational numbers. The proof handles them in the following order.

1.  Section <a href="#sec:expansion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:expansion">2</a> constructs $n+1$ rectangular matrices over $\mathbb Q[t]$. They act on coefficient vectors of polynomials, reversing the Taylor expansion about the distinct points $0,\ldots,n$ and weighting the Taylor coefficients by powers of $t$. A Wronskian bound shows that, for every nonzero subspace of dimension at most $B$, the sum of its images has dimension greater than $n$ times its dimension, even when the subspace has Laurent-series coefficients. The resulting test for a feasible pencil is injective. Padding the test matrices to a common square order $M$ gives matrices $R_i(t)$ for which every feasible pencil of order $1\leq q\leq B$ satisfies $$\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}\Bigl(\sum_{i=0}^n A_i\otimes R_i(t)\Bigr)>(q-1)M.$$

2.  Section <a href="#sec:division" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:division">3</a> uses two further parameters $p,r$ to lift these matrices into a division algebra represented by $M\times M$ matrices. Ranks of block matrices over this algebra are multiples of $M$, so the strict inequality forces full rank. Dividing on the left by the lifted $R_0$ restores the constant term of an affine pencil. A separate application of the lift turns any defined nonzero formula evaluation into a defined invertible one over an extension field. This supplies the feasibility assertion needed for the certificates in Section <a href="#sec:encoding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:encoding">4</a>.

3.  Section <a href="#sec:generator" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:generator">5</a> represents multiplication in a finite-dimensional rational algebra by rational matrices, thereby replacing the root of unity used in the lift. All root components must be invertible simultaneously. For any fixed formula, the product of their determinant conditions is a nonzero polynomial of bounded degree in $t,p,r$. A fixed three-dimensional integer grid detects it. Exact rational arithmetic bounds both the generation time and the total binary output length.

The witnesses, subspaces, and formula-specific determinant products in this proof certify the construction. None is computed by $G$. In particular, the dimension of an initial witness for a formula has no effect on the generator’s resource bounds.

The construction also hits every feasible rational affine pencil of bounded order; see Corollary <a href="#cor:pencil-hitting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:pencil-hitting">13</a>. In particular, it answers the rational-coefficient version of the black-box <span class="smallcaps">Singular</span> construction question for homogeneous pencils posed by Garg, Gurvits, Oliveira, and Wigderson (Garg et al. 2020, sec. 6, p. 280) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[GGOW20](#ref-GGOW20)]. Section <a href="#sec:rank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:rank">6</a> then treats rectangular affine pencils. Their *noncommutative rank* is their rank over the free skew field. Corollary <a href="#cor:rank-detecting-list" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:rank-detecting-list">15</a> constructs a universal nonadaptive list whose normalized evaluation ranks recover this rank for every rectangular rational affine pencil with prescribed row, column, and variable counts. It permits exact matrix-value or ordinary-rank responses and accounts for their processing costs.

<a id="sec:expansion"></a>
### Rectangular tests from subspace expansion

Fix the variable and formula-size bounds $n,s\geq1$. We construct rectangular matrices $T_0,\ldots,T_n$ such that $\sum_{i=0}^n A_i\otimes T_i$ is injective whenever $A_0+\sum_{i=1}^n A_i x_i$ is a feasible affine pencil of order at most $2s+1$. The construction uses an indeterminate $t$; no specialization of a formal Laurent series will be needed. Set <a id="eq:parameters"></a>$$\label{eq:parameters}
\begin{gathered}
w=n+1,\qquad B=2s+1,\qquad \Delta=2w^3B,\\
M=\min\{2^a:a\in\mathbb Z_{\geq0},\ 2^a>B\Delta\},
\qquad N=M-\Delta.
\end{gathered}$$ The bound $B$ will accommodate all certificate pencils in Proposition <a href="#prop:formula-certificates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:formula-certificates">10</a>. The padding width $\Delta$ provides the expansion margin, while $M>B\Delta$ makes the eventual rank deficit smaller than one block. These choices give $1\leq N<M\leq2B\Delta$.

For a field $K$ and an integer $d\geq 1$, let $$\mathcal P_d(K)=\{f\in K[z]:\deg f<d\},$$ with its monomial basis. Put $F=\mathbb Q((t))$ and $\mathcal O=\mathbb Q[[t]]$. For $0\leq i\leq n$, define an $F$-linear map $T_i\colon\mathcal P_N(F)\longrightarrow\mathcal P_M(F)$ by <a id="eq:rectangular-test"></a>$$f=\sum_{a=0}^{N-1}c_{ia}(f)(z-i)^a,
  \qquad
  T_i f=\sum_{a=0}^{N-1}c_{ia}(f)t^a(z-i)^{M-1-a}.
  \label{eq:rectangular-test}$$ The exponents in the output are nonnegative and distinct. Since each $t^a$ is nonzero in $F$, every $T_i$ is injective. Its matrix in the monomial bases has entries in $\mathbb Z[t]$. More explicitly, its column corresponding to $z^b$, for $0\leq b<N$, is the coefficient vector of <a id="eq:test-column"></a>$$T_i(z^b)=\sum_{a=0}^{b}\binom{b}{a}i^{b-a}t^a(z-i)^{M-1-a}.
  \label{eq:test-column}$$ Thus all its entries have $t$-degree at most $N-1$. Let $R_i(t)$ be the $M\times M$ matrix obtained by appending $M-N$ zero columns to $T_i$.

The conclusion of this section is the following proposition.

<a id="stmt-prop-rectangular"></a>
<div id="prop:rectangular" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:padded-rank"></a>
**Proposition 2** (Rectangular rank bound). *Let $1\leq q\leq B$ and $A_0,\ldots,A_n\in\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_q(\mathbb Q)$. Suppose that, for some extension field $E/\mathbb Q$, some integer $e\geq1$, and some $X_1,\ldots,X_n\in\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_e(E)$, the matrix $$A_0\otimes I_e+\sum_{i=1}^n A_i\otimes X_i$$ is invertible. Then $\sum_{i=0}^n A_i\otimes T_i$ is injective over $F$, and $$\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}_{\mathbb Q(t)}\left(\sum_{i=0}^n A_i\otimes R_i(t)\right)
  \geq qN>(q-1)M.
  \label{eq:padded-rank}$$*

</div>

We prove the proposition by showing that the maps $T_i$ jointly expand every nonzero subspace of dimension at most $B$. Two elementary facts will control the vanishing orders of their leading coefficients. The ordinary-Wronskian multiplicity method used in the subspace designs of Guruswami and Kopparty (Guruswami and Kopparty 2016, sec. 5.1 of the preprint) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[GK16](#ref-GK16)] is a predecessor of this argument. Their construction bounds intersections with multiplicity subspaces; here Taylor reversal relates the vanishing sequences of the input and image spaces. Forbes and Guruswami (Forbes and Guruswami 2015) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[FG15](#ref-FG15)] explain broader connections among subspace designs, rank condensers, and dimension expansion. The lossless and unbalanced dimension expanders of Guruswami, Resch, and Xing (Guruswami, Resch, and Xing 2018) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[GRX18](#ref-GRX18)] provided a comparison for the required small-subspace expansion parameters during development. Their linearized-polynomial construction is not used here. We prove the order estimate and the rectangular expansion statement directly for the maps above.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Vanishing orders and integral reduction

<a id="eq:vanishing-filtration"></a>
Let $V\subseteq\mathcal P_d(\mathbb Q)$ have dimension $h\geq1$. Row reduction of Taylor coefficient vectors about $z=i$ gives a basis with distinct vanishing orders $$0\leq d_{i1}<\cdots<d_{ih}<d.$$ These orders describe the entire vanishing filtration: for every integer $r\geq0$, $$\dim_{\mathbb Q}\{f\in V:(z-i)^r\text{ divides }f\}
  =\#\{j:d_{ij}\geq r\}.
  \label{eq:vanishing-filtration}$$ Indeed, in a nonzero linear combination of the echelon basis, the first basis vector with a nonzero coefficient supplies its lowest Taylor term.

The leading-term Wronskian computation in the following lemma is classical; the echelon-basis and Vandermonde argument is also presented by Bostan and Dumas (Bostan and Dumas 2010, Lemmas 1–3) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[BD10](#ref-BD10)].

<a id="stmt-lem-wronskian-orders"></a>
<div id="lem:wronskian-orders" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:wronskian-bound"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Wronskian order bound). *With the notation above, at the $w=n+1$ points $0,\ldots,n$ one has $$\sum_{i=0}^n\sum_{j=1}^h d_{ij}
  \leq h(d-1)+w\binom{h}{2}.
  \label{eq:wronskian-bound}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-wronskian-orders"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-wronskian-orders</summary>

*Proof.* For a basis $f_1,\ldots,f_h$ of $V$, consider its Wronskian $$\mathcal W(z)=\det\bigl(f_j^{(r)}(z)\bigr)_{
       0\leq r<h,\,1\leq j\leq h}.$$ Changing the basis multiplies $\mathcal W$ by a nonzero rational constant. At a fixed point $i$, use the echelon basis and write $f_j=\alpha_j(z-i)^{d_{ij}}+$ higher powers, where $\alpha_j\neq0$. The contribution of these leading terms to the determinant is $$\left(\prod_{j=1}^h\alpha_j\right)
  \det\bigl((d_{ij})_r\bigr)_{
       0\leq r<h,\,1\leq j\leq h}
  (z-i)^{\sum_jd_{ij}-\binom h2},$$ where $(X)_r=X(X-1)\cdots(X-r+1)$ and $(X)_0=1$. Because these are monic polynomials of successive degrees, the displayed coefficient determinant is the Vandermonde $\prod_{a<b}(d_{ib}-d_{ia})$, which is nonzero. Terms using a higher power from any $f_j$ have strictly larger total exponent, or vanish on differentiation. The displayed exponent is nonnegative because the $d_{ij}$ are distinct nonnegative integers. Consequently $$\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_{z=i}\mathcal W
  =\sum_{j=1}^h d_{ij}-\binom h2,$$ and in particular $\mathcal W$ is not the zero polynomial.

Each entry in its determinant has degree at most $d-1$, so $\deg\mathcal W\leq h(d-1)$. The sum of its vanishing orders at the distinct points $0,\ldots,n$ is at most its degree. This gives <a href="#eq:wronskian-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wronskian-bound">[eq:wronskian-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

For a subspace $U\subseteq F^D$, define its reduction by $$\overline U
  =\{u\bmod t:u\in U\cap\mathcal O^D\}\subseteq\mathbb Q^D.$$ It is important here to use all integral vectors of the subspace, not merely the reductions of an arbitrarily chosen basis.

<a id="stmt-lem-integral-reduction"></a>
<div id="lem:integral-reduction" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:integral-lattice"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Integral bases of Laurent-series subspaces). *Every $k$-dimensional subspace $U\subseteq F^D$ has a basis $u_1,\ldots,u_k\in\mathcal O^D$ whose reductions are linearly independent. For such a basis, $$U\cap\mathcal O^D=\bigoplus_{j=1}^k\mathcal O u_j,
  \qquad
  \dim_{\mathbb Q}\overline U=k.
  \label{eq:integral-lattice}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-integral-reduction"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-integral-reduction</summary>

*Proof.* We first construct the basis by induction on $k$, the case $k=0$ being immediate. Multiply a nonzero vector of $U$ by a power of $t$ so that all its coordinates are integral and some coordinate is a unit. Call this vector $u_1$ and its unit coordinate $c$. The subspace of $U$ whose $c$-th coordinate is zero has dimension $k-1$. By induction it has an integral basis with independent reductions. All those reductions have zero $c$-th coordinate, whereas the reduction of $u_1$ does not. Together they are therefore independent and form a basis of $U$ over $F$.

Some $k\times k$ coordinate minor of the resulting basis matrix is a unit in $\mathcal O$. If an $F$-linear combination of its columns is integral, restriction to those coordinates and inversion of that minor show that all its coefficients belong to $\mathcal O$. This proves the first assertion of <a href="#eq:integral-lattice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:integral-lattice">[eq:integral-lattice]</a>; reducing its coefficients modulo $t$ proves the second. ◻

</details>

For a nonzero vector $f$ with Laurent-series coordinates, let $\nu_t(f)$ denote the minimum of their $t$-valuations. Its *leading reduction* is the nonzero vector $t^{-\nu_t(f)}f\bmod t$. Any invertible coordinate change over $\mathbb Q$ preserves both the integral lattice and this minimum valuation: the change-of-basis matrix and its inverse both have entries in $\mathcal O$. In particular, this applies to conversion between monomial and Taylor coordinates. All these statements concern formal series and require no convergence assumption.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Expansion of every small subspace

For an integral input having a unit Taylor coefficient with index $a\leq A$, the leading reduction of its image uses only indices $a\leq A$; reversal turns these into output orders at least $M-1-A$. The Wronskian bound then rules out too small a common image space. The proof below applies this observation to a suitable subspace for each vanishing order.

<a id="stmt-lem-small-expansion"></a>
<div id="lem:small-expansion" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Small-subspace expansion). *For every $k$-dimensional subspace $U\subseteq\mathcal P_N(F)$ with $1\leq k\leq B$, <a id="eq:small-expansion"></a>$$\dim_F\left(\sum_{i=0}^n T_iU\right)>(w-1)k.
  \label{eq:small-expansion}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-small-expansion"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-small-expansion</summary>

<a id="eq:order-reversal"></a>
*Proof.* Put $W=\sum_{i=0}^nT_iU$ and $\ell=\dim_FW$. Since $T_0$ is injective, $\ell\geq k$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:integral-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:integral-reduction">4</a>, $\overline U\subseteq\mathcal P_N(\mathbb Q)$ has dimension $k$ and $\overline W\subseteq\mathcal P_M(\mathbb Q)$ has dimension $\ell$. Write their increasing vanishing orders at $i$ as $$a_{i1}<\cdots<a_{ik},
  \qquad
  b_{i1}<\cdots<b_{i\ell}.$$ We claim that, for every $i$ and every $1\leq j\leq k$, $$b_{i,\ell-j+1}\geq M-1-a_{ij}.
  \label{eq:order-reversal}$$

Fix $i,j$ and set $A=a_{ij}$. Projection of $\overline U$ onto its Taylor coefficients of orders $0,\ldots,A$ has rank $j$. Choose $j$ vectors in $\overline U$ whose projections are independent, lift them to integral vectors of $U$, and let $U'$ be their $F$-span. Their reductions are independent, so Lemma <a href="#lem:integral-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:integral-reduction">4</a> shows that these reductions form a basis of $\overline{U'}$. The chosen Taylor projection is therefore injective on $\overline{U'}$.

Take a nonzero $f\in U'$ and multiply it by a power of $t$ so that $\nu_t(f)=0$. All its Taylor coefficients $c_{ia}(f)$ are integral. Because its nonzero reduction has a nonzero low Taylor projection, at least one coefficient with $a\leq A$ is a unit. With the convention $\nu_t(0)=+\infty$, put $$m=\min_{0\leq a<N}\bigl(\nu_t(c_{ia}(f))+a\bigr).$$ Then $m\leq A$, whereas for every $a>A$ one has $\nu_t(c_{ia}(f))+a\geq a>A\geq m$. Thus the leading reduction of $T_i f$ uses only output powers $(z-i)^{M-1-a}$ with $a\leq A$. These powers are independent, so their leading coefficients cannot all cancel. Since Taylor coordinate changes preserve minimum valuation, $m$ is also the minimum valuation of the output in monomial coordinates. Its leading reduction consequently vanishes at $i$ to order at least $M-1-A$. Figure <a href="#fig:taylor-reversal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:taylor-reversal">1</a> illustrates this reversal.

<figure id="fig:taylor-reversal">

<figcaption>Taylor reversal in the expansion argument (schematic, not to scale). For a normalized input with integral Taylor coefficients and a unit among $a ≤ A$, every term surviving in the leading reduction of its image has $a ≤ A$. The reduction therefore vanishes to order at least $M − 1 − A$ at $i$. The proof applies this to every vector in a $j$-dimensional subspace, with $A = a_{ij}$.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-taylor-reversal"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:taylor-reversal`)

 \draw (\x,-.08)--(\x,.08);
 \node[above=4pt] at (\x,0) {$\lab$};
}

\node[below=4pt] at (0,-2) {$\Delta$};
\draw (3,-2.08)--(3,-1.92);
\node[below=4pt] at (3,-2) {$M-1-A$};
\draw (6.3,-2.08)--(6.3,-1.92);

Visual witness description of PDF page 7 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

Above the axes is the expression $c_a(z - i)^a \mapsto c_a t^a (z - i)^{M-1-a}$ and text: "A unit coefficient with $a \le A$ forces minimum $t$-valuation $\le A$." The top axis is labeled $a$ with tick marks at $0$, $A$, and $N - 1$, with a label above the interval $[0, A]$ reading "possible surviving indices".
